The impact of sports participation on psychological health and social outcomes in children and adolescents: a systematic review and update to the “Mental Health through Sport” conceptual model
Authors: Levi Wade, Rochelle Eime, Aurélie Pankowiak, Stewart Vella, Katie Robinson, Sarah Kennedy, Rebekah Parkes, Narelle Eather
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
BackgroundSport is a distinct, structured form of physical activity that can support psychological health and social development in children and adolescents. Compared to other forms of physical activity, the unique attributes of sport, including its combination of physical, cognitive, and social opportunities and challenges within structured goal-directed contexts, may confer additional psychological and social benefits. This systematic review synthesizes evidence on the psychological and social outcomes of sport participation in children and adolescents, and proposes an updated, developmentally informed conceptual model explaining the mechanisms through which sport supports mental health.MethodsA systematic search was conducted across five electronic databases on 18 October 2024, with no date limits applied. Eligible studies were peer-reviewed, published in English, and focused on the relationship between sport participation and psychological and/or social outcomes in 5–17-year olds. Sport was defined as a structured form of physical activity performed individually or in teams, with established rules and a specific objective. Risk of bias was assessed using validated tools tailored to study design.ResultsFrom 17,017 records, 189 studies were included, spanning cross-sectional, longitudinal, and experimental designs. Sport participation was consistently associated with better mental health—including higher self-esteem, life satisfaction, and reduced depressive and anxiety symptoms—and enhanced social outcomes, such as improved interpersonal skills, prosocial behavior, and belonging. Team sports showed stronger and more consistent benefits than individual sports. The experimental evidence highlighted potential causal effects on emotional regulation, resilience, and prosocial behavior. Risk of bias was generally low among randomized trials, although most relied on self-report data and lacked pre-registration. Observational and quasi-experimental studies were methodologically sound but were affected by potential confounding and social desirability bias.ConclusionsThis review provides compelling evidence that organized sport participation supports psychological and social development in children and adolescents. Sport was consistently associated with higher self-esteem, greater life satisfaction, and lower symptoms of depression and anxiety, with team sports showing stronger and more consistent benefits than individual sports. Social outcomes—including enhanced belonging, prosocial behavior, and interpersonal skills—were also evident, underscoring the unique contribution of sport as a social context for development. The refined conceptual model proposed here identifies self-perceptions, peer belonging, and social support as central mechanisms, offering a developmentally informed framework for leveraging sport to promote youth mental health. These findings highlight sport, especially inclusive and well-structured team sports, as a powerful setting for fostering psychological well-being and positive social outcomes, warranting prioritization in youth development policies and programs.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1186/s13643-026-03104-1.

Introduction

Sport is a structured mode of physical activity that requires participants to adhere to predefined rules and objectives, such as scoring points, completing a set distance in the shortest time, or outperforming an opponent in a competitive setting [1]. Sport-related activities generally fall into two categories: organized (e.g., playing volleyball with a club in a local competition, swimming with a squad), or informal (e.g., playing backyard cricket with the family, surfing for fitness on your own) [2]—with organized sport being the focus of this review. Individuals may engage in organized sport training and/or competition through various modalities, including socially oriented activities (e.g., social pickleball competitions), organized team or individual competitions and events (e.g., football tournaments or triathlon races), or structured classes (e.g., martial arts or dance lessons), with participation levels ranging from sport delivered in the school setting, through to elite competitions [3]. While all forms of physical activity can support physical and mental health, sport is distinct from recreational activities (e.g., walking, cycling) and structured exercise (e.g., gym-based workouts) because it combines cognitive engagement, social interaction, emotional regulation, and goal-directed movement within the context of sport-specific rules and objectives [1, 4]. Each of these elements may contribute to the distinct psychological and social benefits often observed with young sports participants [5]. Building on our recent systematic review of sport participation in adults [6], this review synthesizes evidence on the psychological and social outcomes of sport participation in children and adolescents, and examines potential mechanisms and moderators underpinning these associations.

Sports participation and mental health in youth

Mental health encompasses psychological, emotional, and social outcomes. Psychological outcomes are often conceptualized through two broad indicators: psychological well-being (e.g., self-esteem, resilience, life satisfaction) and psychological ill-being (e.g., depressive symptoms, anxiety, psychological distress) [7, 8]. Emotional outcomes relate to an individual’s capacity to experience, regulate, and express emotions effectively [9], and social outcomes pertain to how an individual interacts in their social environments, inclusive of relationships, community engagement, and social support networks [10]. Within this social domain, social well-being describes one’s perceptions of their social relationships and their roles within society, and prosocial behaviors refer to the voluntary actions of an individual which are intended to benefit others [11].

In sporting contexts, prosocial behaviors can be taught, modeled, and reinforced (e.g., encouraging others, cooperating with teammates, volunteering at the club, and helping an injured player). Higher levels of social well-being and prosocial behavior have been linked to better mental health, including lower levels of depression and anxiety [12, 13], as well as improved social outcomes such as greater empathy, peer relationships, social competence, and connectedness [11, 14]. Notably, these two social concepts are also viewed as key mediating mechanisms through which sport participation may enhance mental health [5, 15, 16]. These findings underscore the importance of fostering social connections and encouraging prosocial behaviors as strategies to promote mental health and well-being in youth through sport.

Over the past two decades, a growing body of research has explored how physical activity—and particularly sports participation—shapes mental and social outcomes in young people [17]. Early work by Eime et al. (2013) synthesized evidence demonstrating that youth sports participation is associated with higher self-esteem, improved peer relationships, and lower depressive symptoms. Their findings also suggested that sport may confer stronger mental health benefits than other forms of physical activity, potentially because it is typically organized and structured nature, involves sustained social interaction with peers, and offers ample opportunities for skill mastery.

Since then, research in this area has expanded, with longitudinal and experimental studies reinforcing and extending these findings, and offering support for directional and causal interpretations [16, 18, 19]. Critical reviews, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses further indicate that team sports are consistently linked to improved psychological well-being and lower psychological distress, whereas findings for individual sports are more variable [5, 16, 19–22]. Evidence also supports that sports participation is associated with improved social competence and prosocial behaviors, further emphasizing its role in fostering positive developmental outcomes [23–26]. At the same time, research has documented negative experiences in sport, including instances of psychological, physical, and sexual abuse of young athletes, highlighting the need for context-specific safeguards and effective child protection practices [27–29].

Nevertheless, across the literature, there is relatively consistent evidence that sport participation can support young people’s mental health; however, the mechanisms underlying these effects have been underexplored. Clarifying these mechanisms is essential for optimizing the design and implementation of sport-based interventions that effectively enhance mental health in children and adolescents [30]. Studies with adults have helped researchers identify several key pathways through which sport may influence mental health, including physical activity-related mechanisms such as improved self-perceptions and neurobiological adaptations, as well as social mechanisms such as belonging, peer support, and role-based purpose [16]. However, it remains unclear whether these same mechanisms apply to children and adolescents or whether developmental differences—cognitive, emotional, social, and physical—shape how sport influences mental health in younger populations [5, 11].

This systematic review examines the relationship between sports participation and psychological and social outcomes in children and adolescents, focusing on psychological well-being, psychological ill-being, prosocial behaviors, and antisocial behaviors. It also identifies key mediators that may explain how sport influences mental health in young people. Drawing on the full body of evidence, we refine existing conceptual models linking sport to mental health outcomes to better reflect the developmental factors shaping these associations in youth. This review provides a comprehensive framework for understanding the role of sport in promoting mental health and social development, and may inform future research, programs, and policies aimed at maximizing these benefits.

Methods

Our systematic review was prospectively registered with PROSPERO (CRD42023452595) and carried out following the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses (PRISMA) recommendations [31]. The PRISMA checklist is attached as Appendix 1.

The first author (LW) conducted a systematic search of five electronic databases (Cochrane Library, MEDLINE, PsycINFO, SPORT Discus, and Web of Science) in October 2024. The keywords used in the search were as follows: sport* AND child* OR adol* OR student* AND well-being OR wellbeing OR “mental health” OR “quality of life” OR life satisfaction OR anxiety OR depression OR happiness OR self-efficacy OR self-esteem OR “positive youth development” OR stress OR internalizing OR externalizing OR vitality OR self-confidence OR belonging OR bullying OR victim* OR prosocial OR sociability OR “social interaction” OR interpersonal OR “social capital” OR aggression. Medical Subject Headings (MeSH) were applied where possible. Results were limited to studies published in English and in a peer-reviewed journal. No limitations on publication date were applied during the search. The full search strategy is available in Appendix 2.

Criteria for inclusion/exclusion

To be included in this review, each study needed to meet the following criteria: (1) published in English; (2) full-text available online; (3) original quantitative research or report published in a peer-reviewed journal (including cross-sectional, longitudinal, quasi-experimental, or experimental designs); (4) provides data on the psychological or social effects of participation in sport, with sport defined as a subset of exercise that can be undertaken individually or as a part of a team played at any level (recreational, community, regionally or nationally representative, elite), and where participants adhere to a common set of rules or expectations, and a defined goal exists; and (5) the population of interest had a mean age between five and 17 years (child and/or adolescent populations), who were otherwise healthy. This upper age boundary differed slightly from our PROSPERO registration (5–18 years); we decided to apply 5–17 years to align with our focus on children and adolescents and with the age range used in the evidence underpinning the 2020 WHO guidelines on physical activity and sedentary behavior for children and adolescents [32]. Eligibility was first assessed by screening titles and abstracts of all records retrieved in the initial search. Full-text articles were then reviewed independently by two authors. Reference lists of included studies were also examined to identify additional relevant research. When full texts were not readily accessible, we attempted to obtain them by contacting corresponding authors; however, contact attempts were not systematically logged.

Data extraction and synthesis

Data extraction was performed by two authors (LW, KR) using the Covidence systematic review software (Veritas Health Innovation, Melbourne, Australia). Extracted data included the following: author, year of publication, age, country in which the study was run, design, aim, details of conditions (e.g., sport intervention dose), outcomes of interest, outcome measures, sample size, male/female ratio, sport/s of interest, measure of effect/ratio/correlation of sport participation and outcome, included covariates, and key findings. Meta-analysis was planned a priori; however, it was not undertaken because studies reported outcomes using diverse measurement tools and effect metrics, often with different adjustment sets, which precluded meaningful pooling across outcomes and study designs. Findings were synthesized narratively, with studies grouped by the four outcome domains (psychological well-being, psychological ill-being, prosocial behavior, and antisocial behavior). In addition to summarizing main effects, we extracted and reported evidence on potential mediators (e.g., social connectedness, self-perceptions) and moderators (e.g., sex, sport type) of these associations to help elucidate underlying mechanisms and contextual factors. Mediation results were extracted only when mediation was explicitly tested and reported by study authors, and we recorded whether mediation was examined using univariate or multivariable models, as described in the primary studies.

Risk of bias assessment

A risk of bias assessment was performed at the study level by two authors (SK and RP) independently. Randomized trials were assessed using the Cochrane risk-of-bias tool for randomized trials (RoB 2) [33], non-randomized trials were assessed using the Risk Of Bias In Non-randomized Studies of Interventions (ROBINS-I) [34], and observational studies were assessed using the National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI) Quality Assessment Tool for Observational Cohort and Cross-Sectional Studies [35]. Discrepancies between reviewers were resolved through discussion with a third author (LW) when needed.

Results

Overview of included studies

The systematic search yielded 17,017 articles (Fig. 1), which were imported into the Covidence systematic review software for screening. After duplicate removal, 14,079 unique studies were screened by title and abstract. Of these, 729 studies were assessed for eligibility through full-text review, conducted independently by two authors (LW and NE), with RE resolving any disagreements. A total of 189 studies were included in the current systematic review. Life satisfaction

(Satisfaction with life scale)

1.Fernandez, 2024 [43]

2. Cross-sectional

1.Organized sport

2. Both

Both team and individual sports participation were associated with higher levels of use of emotion in comparison to non-participants, whereas team sport participants also revealed better levels of self-emotional appraisal, regulation of emotion, and self-esteem

Mediation—emotional intelligence dimensions (self-emotional appraisal and use of emotion) and self-esteem play chain mediating roles between youth sports participation and life satisfaction

1.Gadermann, 2016 [50]

2. Cross-sectional

1.Individual and team sports

2. Both

Participating in team sports was related to higher life satisfaction

Moderation – team, not individual sports was associated with life satisfaction

1.Guddal, 2019 [51]

2. Cross-sectional

1.Team or individual sport

2. Both

Participation in team sports was associated with reduced odds of low life satisfaction, among all girls and among junior high school boys

Moderation – team, not individual sports was associated with life satisfaction

1.Perron, 2012 [156]

2. Longitudinal (2 years)

1.Sports

2. Both

Participation in team sports (but not in individual sports) interacted with peer victimization in predicting the extent of concurrent depressive symptoms and subsequent externalizing problems. Victimized children who rarely participated in team sports had a higher level of teacher-rated depressive symptoms at age 8 compared to victimized children who were frequently enrolled in such activities

Moderation – Only positive associations in team sports

Moderation – victimized children who did not participate in sport had higher depressive symptoms

1.Perron-Gelinas, 2017 [157]

2. Longitudinal (1 year)

1.Sport

2. Both

The frequency of participation in sports interacted with depressive symptoms in predicting peer rejection levels one year later, albeit differently for team sports and individual sports

Moderation—even high levels of depressive symptoms did not predict increased peer rejection for boys who often participated in team sports. A very different picture emerged for boys with elevated depressive symptoms who often participated in individual sports, however, who became even more rejected over time

Summary of mediators

The included studies were published between 1992 and 2024, with a noticeable increase in publications in recent years. Cross-sectional designs were the most common (n = 121), followed by longitudinal studies (n = 50) and experimental studies (n = 18). Sample sizes varied widely, ranging from 20 to 70,499 participants. The populations studied predominantly comprised adolescents aged 13–17 years (n = 103), followed by children aged 5–12 years (n = 51), and a smaller number of studies spanned both age groups (n = 35).

The most frequently assessed outcomes were depressive symptoms or depression (n = 60), self-esteem or self-worth (n = 35), and anxiety or symptoms of anxiety (n = 29). Most studies did not focus on specific sports but instead compared sport participation to general physical activity or inactivity, or contrasted individual and team sports in relation to mental health and social outcomes. Experimental studies more often evaluated specific sports (e.g., basketball, soccer, judo), with martial arts programs—such as judo, karate, taekwondo, and Brazilian jiu-jitsu—accounting for 72% of the 18 experimental interventions.

The findings have been synthesized into four key categories: (i) psychological well-being; (ii) psychological ill-being; (iii) prosocial behavior; and (iv) antisocial behavior.

Psychological well-being

Overall, the evidence in this review indicates that this review provides consistent evidence that sports participation is positively associated with psychological well-being in children and adolescents. Across 88 studies (62 cross-sectional, 16 longitudinal, and 10 experimental), sports participation was linked to higher self-esteem, life satisfaction, positive body image, resilience, and overall psychological well-being. The strongest evidence emerged for self-esteem and self-worth, with 35 studies (22 cross-sectional, 9 longitudinal, and 4 experimental) consistently demonstrating that sports participation fosters self-esteem and self-worth. Team sports were more beneficial than individual sports, likely due to their more socially embedded nature, and several studies highlighted mechanisms underpinning these benefits, including sport self-concept [108], peer acceptance [104], and adult support [98, 100].

Longitudinal studies reinforced these findings, showing that sports participation predicts self-esteem, with additional evidence supporting bidirectional effects [107, 213]. Mediators such as sport self-concept [108, 110] and peer belonging [54] helped explain how sport contributes to self-esteem and overall well-being. There was also strong longitudinal evidence supporting the positive role of sports in improving health-related quality of life in both children and adolescents [18, 72, 73].

Findings for emotional regulation were more mixed. Martial arts interventions [78, 194] demonstrated some benefits for emotional regulation, particularly for taekwondo, whereas other judo and ball sports interventions showed no significant effects [45]. As shown in Table 3, the mechanisms driving the effects on psychological well-being include improvements in self-perceptions, such as body satisfaction, physical self-concept, and self-esteem, which frequently mediated the impact of sports on broader psychological outcomes. Social factors, including peer belonging and social integration, were also critical in explaining improvements in life satisfaction and well-being, further emphasizing the role of team sports in fostering positive social experiences.

Body image and body satisfaction (6 studies: 6 cross-sectional)

All six studies examining body image and body satisfaction outcomes were cross-sectional. Five studies in adolescents reported positive associations between sport participation and body satisfaction, whereas the single study conducted in children found no association [39]. The limited and predominantly adolescent-focused evidence makes it difficult to draw firm conclusions for younger children, and suggests that age-related differences in body image processes warrant further investigation.

Emotional regulation (4 studies: 1 cross-sectional, 3 experimental)

Three experimental studies, all focusing on martial arts interventions (karate, judo, taekwondo), assessed the effects of sports on emotional regulation [43–45]. While the cross-sectional study found a positive association [42], only the taekwondo intervention among the experimental studies demonstrated improvements in emotional regulation [44].

Life satisfaction (11 studies: 10 cross-sectional, 1 longitudinal)

The findings consistently indicate that sports participation is associated with higher life satisfaction (10/11 studies). The evidence from the lone longitudinal study suggested that transitioning from non-participation to team sports over 3 years improves life satisfaction, and was mediated by increased peer belonging [152].

Psychological well-being (12 studies: 9 cross-sectional, 3 longitudinal)

The evidence consistently supports a positive association between sports participation and overall psychological well-being (10/12 studies). Longitudinal evidence suggests that ongoing sport engagement predicts improvements in well-being over time [25, 65].

Quality of life (11 studies: 7 cross-sectional, 3 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

Observational evidence consistently links sports participation to a higher rating of quality of life. Longitudinal findings demonstrate that sports participation leads to improvements in quality of life rather than being a consequence of it [18, 73]. However, the only experimental study involving adolescents, which assessed a Muay Thai intervention, resulted in no significant effects [74].

Resilience (5 studies: 3 cross-sectional, 2 experimental)

Cross-sectional studies consistently found that sports participation is associated with greater resilience, particularly for those engaged in team sports or higher-frequency participation [75–77]. Experimental evidence was mixed, with a martial arts program [78] demonstrating significant improvements on ratings of resilience, whereas a soccer-based intervention [79] showed no effect.

Self-concept and physical self-concept (4 studies: 4 cross-sectional)

The evidence suggests that sports participation positively influences self-concept and physical self-concept [80–83], which may serve as important mechanisms for broader psychological outcomes, including self-esteem [81, 82].

Self-esteem and self-worth (35 studies: 22 cross-sectional, 9 longitudinal, 4 experimental)

The findings overwhelmingly support the positive effects of sports participation on self-esteem and self-worth. Consistent cross-sectional and longitudinal evidence demonstrate that sports participation predicts and improves self-esteem, with experimental studies reinforcing these effects. Mechanisms such as sport self-concept [108, 110], peer acceptance [104], and adult support [98] highlight the pathways through which sports participation fosters self-esteem. Team sports consistently provided greater benefits for self-esteem than individual sports [43, 51, 108, 109], underscoring the importance of social interaction in driving these effects.

Psychological ill-being

A total of 150 studies (108 cross-sectional, 36 longitudinal, 6 experimental) examined the relationship between sports participation and psychological ill-being outcomes, including anxiety, depression, externalizing and internalizing symptoms, mental health difficulties, psychological stress, social anxiety, and suicidal ideation. The evidence consistently suggests that sports, particularly team sports, play a protective role across many psychological ill-being outcomes, though findings vary in strength and consistency depending on the outcome.

Depressive symptoms were the most studied, with 60 studies (43 cross-sectional, 16 longitudinal, 1 experimental) consistently showing that sports participation, particularly team sports, is associated with fewer depressive symptoms. Longitudinal and experimental studies, including those conducted by Briere, Yale-Souliere [153], Gomez-Baya, Mendoza [38], and Roh, Cho and So [159], demonstrate that participation in sports predict sustained reductions in depressive symptoms. Several mechanisms, including self-esteem [84, 131], body satisfaction [38], peer belonging [54], and social integration [209], help explain this relationship. Importantly, these benefits persist in studies that controlled for physical activity [113, 138, 214], indicating that features of the sporting context—such as its social, identity-related, and self-evaluative dimensions—may be central to the mental health benefits observed.

Similarly, 29 studies (24 cross-sectional, 5 longitudinal) provide consistent support for the protective effects of sports participation against anxiety. Longitudinal research by Graupensperger, Sutcliffe, and Vella [128] demonstrates that team sports predict long-term reductions in anxiety, with peer belonging emerging as a key mediator. These benefits also remain in studies that controlled for physical activity [72, 113, 214].

For other ill-being outcomes, evidence was also generally positive. There is consistent evidence that sports participation is associated with lower internalizing symptoms and improved mental health, with all four longitudinal studies showing sustained mental health benefits across 6 months [65], 1 year [59, 64], and up to 8 years [18].

Anxiety (29 studies: 24 cross-sectional, 5 longitudinal)

The evidence consistently supports the protective effects of sports participation, particularly team sports, in reducing anxiety. Longitudinal findings highlight the sustained benefits of team sports participation throughout adolescence, with peer belonging emerging as a mediator [82]. These benefits persist even after controlling for physical activity [72, 113, 214].

Depression (60 studies: 43 cross-sectional, 16 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

The findings for depressive symptoms are particularly robust. Sixty studies, comprising 43 cross-sectional, 16 longitudinal, and one experimental study, consistently indicate that sports participation, especially team sports, is associated with reductions in depressive symptoms. Longitudinal studies, including Briere, Yale-Souliere [153], and Gomez-Baya, Mendoza [38], and a randomized trial by Roh, Cho, and So [159] demonstrate that team sports participation predicts sustained improvements in depressive symptoms. Mediators such as self-esteem [84, 131], body satisfaction [38], belonging and companionship [54, 133, 152, 209], and social support [131] are instrumental in explaining this relationship. Notably, several studies reported that these benefits persisted even after adjusting for physical activity [113, 138, 214], underscoring the distinct psychological and social mechanisms of sport.

Externalizing symptoms (12 studies: 6 cross-sectional, 5 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

Findings for externalizing symptoms were mixed, though no studies suggested that sports increased externalizing behaviors. Some studies found reductions in externalizing symptoms, particularly in organized or non-contact sports [160, 162]. A large cross-sectional study involving 1816 adolescents participating in individual and/or team sports provided important insights, with the authors reporting that affiliation with prosocial peers may buffer against externalizing behaviors, while association with risk-taking peers may increase them [173].

Internalizing symptoms (10 studies: 6 cross-sectional, 4 longitudinal)

The evidence indicates that sports participation, particularly team sports, may lead to lower internalizing symptoms. Three out of four longitudinal studies reported reductions in internalizing symptoms linked to consistent sports participation, especially in team sports [25, 162, 164]. Cross-sectional findings are mixed, with some studies highlighting benefits for boys and those in team or outdoor sports [121, 160] while others found no significant associations [90, 116].

Mental health (17 studies: 10 cross-sectional, 4 longitudinal, 3 experimental)

All cross-sectional and longitudinal studies supported a positive relationship between sports participation and better mental health outcomes, with longitudinal evidence demonstrating sustained benefits over time [18, 174, 175, 215]. Experimental findings were mixed, with a Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu intervention improving mental health [176], while football [79] and karate [177] showed no effect.

Psychological stress (7 studies: 6 cross-sectional, 1 experimental)

Evidence for psychological stress is mixed, with some studies showing that sports participation is associated with lower stress [51, 118], while others found no significant effect [115, 178]. A 12-week tennis intervention significantly reduced stress [179], providing preliminary experimental support.

Social anxiety (6 studies: 2 cross-sectional, 4 longitudinal)

Social anxiety, characterized by fear of social situations, negative self-evaluation, avoidance behaviors, and altered thought patterns [216], was reduced in several longitudinal studies of sport participation, particularly in team sports [105, 153, 182]. Cross-sectional evidence was more variable, with one study finding selective gender differences [49] and another reporting no association [180].

Suicidal ideation (9 studies: all cross-sectional)

Most of the cross-sectional evidence (6 out of 9 studies) supported that team sports participation is associated with lower suicidal ideation. Two studies found stronger associations (i.e., reductions in suicidal ideation) in boys [91, 171]. However, some studies found no significant associations [145, 183, 186], and one study involving 1037 adolescents supported that general physical activity, rather than sports, was linked to lower suicidal ideation [186].

Prosocial outcomes

A total of 42 studies (18 cross-sectional, 16 longitudinal, and 8 experimental) examined the relationship between sports participation and prosocial outcomes, including belonging and companionship, prosocial behavior, social acceptance and support, social competence, and positive youth development. Social outcomes were generally assessed using tools that evaluated broader aspects of social life rather than social outcomes in sport-specific contexts (e.g., Vermont Health and Behavioral Questionnaire, Dutch Health Behavioral Questionnaire). Overall, the findings suggest that sport participation—particularly in team sports—fosters social integration, interpersonal relationships, and prosocial behaviors.

Prosocial behaviors were the most examined outcome (14 studies), followed by social acceptance and support (13 studies). The evidence for prosocial behavior was largely positive, with cross-sectional studies consistently report higher prosocial behavior among sport participants, and three out of five longitudinal studies showing that sports participation predicted increases in prosocial behavior [25, 65, 188]. Experimental findings were mixed but promising—three of six studies showed improvements in prosocial behavior, with interventions in martial arts [44, 159] and soccer [79] demonstrating significant positive effects.

Regarding social acceptance and support, longitudinal evidence consistently showed that team sports participation predicts sustained improvements in social integration, peer support, and social acceptance over time [104, 130, 160]. Daniels and Leaper (2006) showed that increases in peer acceptance through sport participation mediated later improvements in self-esteem, and the basketball program evaluated by Sui et al. (2023) was shown to improve participants’ social adjustment via their development of interpersonal relationships (including relationships with peers, parents, teachers, and strangers).

Findings related to social competence and social self-efficacy were more variable. Cross-sectional research generally supported a positive association, while longitudinal studies found small but consistent increases in perceived social competence [193] or no association [105].

Belonging and companionship (5 studies: 2 cross-sectional, 3 longitudinal)

Sports participation, particularly in team settings, is associated with higher peer belonging and greater social connections in their general life, including connections with adults and school and at home, and with peers or friends [50, 133]. Longitudinal evidence indicates that participating in team sports predicted sustained social integration, which in turn led to lower depressive symptoms [130, 152]. Participation in a 3-week community sports program for adolescents also led to increased sense of community [187].

Prosocial behavior (14 studies: 3 cross-sectional, 5 longitudinal, and 6 experimental)

Evidence for an association between sport participation and prosocial behavior was generally positive, although there was some variation across study designs. Cross-sectional studies consistently reported higher prosocial behavior among sport participants [25, 83, 146]. Longitudinal findings were mixed. Three studies indicated that sports participation predicted improvements in prosocial behaviors over 6 months [65], 1 year [25], and 4 years [188], with participation in team sports being especially beneficial [25, 188]. However, two of the five longitudinal studies found no association between sport participation and prosocial behavior over follow-up periods of 2 [164] and 4 years [162].

The experimental evidence provides an indication that sport can be used to effectively promote prosocial development, though findings were not uniformly positive. Interventions in taekwondo [159, 217] and football [79] led to improvements in prosocial behavior or sociability. However, other trials involving Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu [176], Karate [177], and a separate Taekwondo intervention [189] showed no significant effects. Together, these findings suggest that sport participation can foster prosocial behavior, although the magnitude and consistency of effects may depend on sport type, context, and participant characteristics.

Social acceptance and support (13 studies, 8 cross-sectional, 4 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

Sports participation is consistently associated with greater social acceptance and support, particularly in team-based settings [37, 160, 190]. All of the cross-sectional studies indicated that sports participation is linked to higher social acceptance, which included peer and parental support. One study found that positive experiences in sport acted as a mediator of these effects [37]. Longitudinal findings reinforce this association, showing that continued participation in sports clubs predicts improved social support and peer acceptance over time [64, 104, 160], with one study showing that peer acceptance mediated the link between sports and self-esteem [104]. Experimental evidence presented by Sui, Lu [191], further supports these findings, with a 12-week basketball program enhancing interpersonal relationships and social adjustment in young adolescents.

Positive youth development (1 study: 1 longitudinal)

The lone longitudinal study showed that youth who participated in individual and team sports in combination had more positive outcomes across three years of high school compared to non-participants [151].

Social competence/social self-efficacy (9 studies: 6 cross-sectional, 2 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

Evidence from cross-sectional research suggests that sports participation is associated with higher social competence [83, 90] and social self-concept [85, 94]. Longitudinal research provides mixed findings, with one study showing improvements in social competence over 4 years [193], while another reported no association across a year [105]. The experimental study found that a school-based judo intervention led to significant improvements in social self-concept among secondary school students [194].

Antisocial outcomes

A total of 45 studies (26 cross-sectional, 12 longitudinal, and 7 experimental) investigated the relationship between sports participation and antisocial outcomes, including aggression, bullying, conduct problems, victimization, loneliness, and peer rejection. Overall, the evidence does not point to a simple or consistent association between sport participation and antisocial behaviors; rather, patterns vary across antisocial subdomains and study designs. For aggression and conduct problems in particular, the findings suggest that while sport participation does not generally exacerbate these behaviors, well-structured sport programs may help to reduce them.

Aggression and conduct problems were the most frequently examined outcomes (11 and 10 studies, respectively). Most studies reported no association between sports participation and aggression [105, 121, 123, 196, 218], except for one longitudinal study linking early physical aggression to later team sports involvement [198]. However, experimental studies consistently showed that organized individual and team sports programs, such as martial arts and volleyball, reduced aggression [194, 200, 201].

Findings on conduct problems were more mixed, with two longitudinal studies indicating that sports participation increased non-violent antisocial behaviors [206, 207], particularly when participation involved to unstructured socialization. However, experimental evidence consistently showed that organized sport interventions (e.g., karate, taekwondo, school team sports) reduced conduct problems [91, 177, 189].

For bullying and victimization, four of the eight studies found no association between sports participation and bullying outcomes [160, 202, 204, 205], though one study found that wrestling was associated with higher rates of both perpetration and victimization [203], while martial arts participation was linked to lower bullying behavior [195]. One study conducted by Perron, Brendgen [156], provided longitudinal evidence that participation in team sports may protect against victimization.

Aggression (11 studies: 4 cross-sectional, 3 longitudinal, 4 experimental)

Most cross-sectional [121, 123, 218] and longitudinal studies [196, 213] do not provide evidence to support an association between sports participation and aggression. However, evidence from experimental research supports that organized sport can reduce aggression, with interventions in martial arts [194, 200] and volleyball [201] demonstrating significant reductions in aggressive behavior.

Bullying (6 studies: 5 cross-sectional, 1 longitudinal)

Most cross-sectional and longitudinal studies found no relationship between sports participation and bullying perpetration or victimization [160, 202, 204, 205]. However, wrestling was linked to higher levels of both behaviors [203], while martial arts practitioners exhibited lower bullying behavior [195].

Conduct problems (10 studies: 4 cross-sectional, 4 longitudinal, 2 experimental)

The evidence on conduct problems was mixed. Cross-sectional findings support that team sport participation is associated with fewer social problems [121] and lower engagement in antisocial behaviors such as truancy, physical fighting, and vandalism [91]. By contrast, the included longitudinal research indicates that sports participation, particularly combat and power sports (such as boxing, wrestling, weightlifting), may increase antisocial involvement [206, 207]. However, the experimental evidence found that Karate and Taekwondo programs reduced conduct problems [177, 189].

Loneliness (5 studies: 3 cross-sectional, 2 longitudinal)

The evidence supports that team sports reduce loneliness and social withdrawal, with the three cross-sectional studies consistently linking team sports participation to lower loneliness levels [146, 148, 208]. Longitudinal research evidence confirms these patterns, showing that team sports predict reductions in loneliness over time in adolescents [153] and lower social withdrawal in children [130].

Peer problems (4 studies: 2 cross-sectional, 1 longitudinal, 1 experimental)

Team sports participation was associated with fewer peer problems and lower peer rejection, particularly for boys [120, 172]. Longitudinal evidence demonstrates that team sports can protect against peer rejection, while individual sports are linked to greater peer rejection [157]. Experimental evidence further supports this, with a soccer intervention reducing peer problems in adolescent boys [79].

Victimization (8 studies: 7 cross-sectional, 1 longitudinal)

Most cross-sectional evidence supports that team sports participation reduces victimization [195, 204, 210, 211]. Two of the remaining three cross-sectional papers reported no relationship [202, 205], and one found an increase in the victimization of wrestlers compared to non-practitioners [203]. Longitudinal evidence supports the protective effects of team sports, with Perron, Brendgen [156], demonstrating that participation in team sports at age 8 was associated with lower victimization and fewer externalizing problems 2 years later.

Overview of moderation analysis

A total of 48 studies examined and reported potential moderators of the relationship between sport participation and mental health or social outcomes. Among these, sex and the type of sport (e.g., individual versus team sports) emerged as the most frequently tested moderators, offering valuable insights into the conditions under which sport participation may be most beneficial. Other variables, such as the number of hours of sport participation, were also tested, but too few studies examined these factors to allow for meaningful conclusions.

Sex was examined as a moderator in 16 studies, revealing a complex and sometimes inconsistent pattern. For males, sport participation was linked to lower depressive symptoms [135], lower victimization [211], and lower loneliness at moderate levels of participation [148]—although one study found excessive participation (> 7–10 h/week) to be associated with increased depressive symptoms and loneliness [148]. For females, there was some evidence that sport participation was associated with improved social competence and reduced social problems and rule-breaking [219], as well as protection against depressed mood associated with poor academic performance [154]. In one longitudinal study, participating in both team and individual sports was associated with higher positive youth development for both sexes, though the timing and outcomes differed (e.g., lower depression in boys at grade 10; higher positive development in girls across grades) [151]. Overall, however, the available evidence is not sufficient to provide a clear or consistent account of sex differences, and further work is needed to clarify how participation intensity and developmental stage may differentially shape outcomes for boys and girls.

Sport type (i.e., team or individual) was examined as a moderator in 36 studies, with consistent evidence favoring the benefits of team sports. Across these studies, 33 reported that team sports were more strongly associated with positive mental health and social outcomes than individual sports, suggesting a robust pattern. One longitudinal study of Australian children and adolescents [18] added nuance, finding that participation in both team and individual sports combined provided the greatest benefits. Conversely, another longitudinal study [153] and a cross-sectional study [160] found no significant differences based on sport type. Taken together, these findings provide compelling evidence that team sport participation is particularly beneficial for young people’s mental health and social outcomes, likely reflecting the added value of its inherently social and collaborative nature.

Mediators

Potential mediating mechanisms through which sport participation may influence mental health and social outcomes were examined in 30 analyses across the included studies. These findings are summarized in Table 3 and integrated within the “Mental Health through Sport” conceptual model (Fig. 2). The reported mediators fell into four broad categories: (1) self-perceptions, including mastery and self-esteem [38, 43, 52, 81, 82, 84, 108, 110, 131]; (2) social mechanisms, such as belonging and companionship, social support, social influence, and comparison [54, 98, 100, 104, 130, 131, 133, 152, 161, 212]; (3) neurobiological mechanisms, including changes in brain structure [121, 136]; and other mechanisms, such as affective and emotional processes, and behavior regulation [37, 103, 200]. This final category captured mechanisms that did not align neatly with the self-perception, social, or neurobiological domains but were nonetheless identified as significant mediators of mental health and social outcomes in the included studies. Together, these findings highlight that sport may promote psychological and social outcomes through a combination of self-related, social, biological, and emotional pathways.Fig. 2“Mental Health through Sport” conceptual model for children and adolescents

“Mental Health through Sport” conceptual model for children and adolescents

Risk of bias assessment

Full details of the risk of bias results are provided in Appendix 3. Below is a discussion of the findings for each type of study.

Observational studies

Of the 172 observational studies, most had clear research questions, well-defined populations, and provided specific definitions of sport, often categorized as team or individual activities. Approximately 88% of studies used valid and reliable measures for outcomes. However, the reliance on self-reported data introduced a notable risk of bias, as blinding was generally not possible.

Quasi-experimental studies

The 11 quasi-experimental studies demonstrated a low risk of bias in participant selection, intervention classification, and handling of missing data. However, many did not adequately account for confounding variables such as prior sport participation, and outcome measurement was often prone to bias due to self-reporting, increasing the likelihood of social desirability effects.

Randomized controlled trials

The seven RCTs were consistently conducted with a low risk of bias in the randomization process, intervention assignment, and management of missing data. However, outcome measurement bias remained a concern due to the reliance on self-reported data. Additionally, the absence of pre-registration across all RCTs raised concerns about selective reporting, as it was unclear whether all planned outcomes were included.

Discussion

This systematic review provides the most comprehensive synthesis to date of the relationships between sport participation and psychological well-being, psychological ill-being, prosocial behaviors, and antisocial behaviors in children and adolescents. Overall, the evidence supports that sport participation is associated with improved psychological outcomes, including higher self-esteem, greater life satisfaction, and reduced symptoms of depression and anxiety. Social benefits were also evident, such as improved social behavior, enhanced interpersonal skills, and a stronger sense of belonging. Evidence from longitudinal studies supported the temporal relationship between sport participation and improved mental health and social outcomes, while experimental studies, though fewer in number, provided preliminary support for a causal effect. Across the included studies, team sports regularly emerged as more consistently associated with positive outcomes than individual sports. Building on this evidence, and extending our previous work in adults [6], we propose the refined mental health through sport conceptual model for children and adolescents (Fig. 2). This model integrates the psychological and social mechanisms identified in the literature to illustrate how sport participation can influence mental health and social outcomes in young people. By synthesizing evidence across diverse study designs and outcomes, the model provides a comprehensive framework to inform future research, policy, and practice aimed at creating positive sporting experiences for all participants.

Conceptual model of sports participation and mental health in children and adolescents

The mental health through sport conceptual model, originally developed for adults [6], has been refined here to reflect evidence specific to children and adolescents (Fig. 2). It illustrates the key pathways through which sport participation influences mental health and social outcomes in young people, integrating findings from the studies included in this systematic review.

The model builds upon previous theoretical frameworks while being directly informed by the body of research synthesized in this review. It conceptualizes the physical and social components of sport as distinct but interconnected contributors to a range of psychological and social outcomes, including psychological well-being, psychological ill-being, prosocial behaviors, and antisocial behaviors. By integrating empirical findings from youth populations, this model refines our understanding of the specific mechanisms through which sports participation influences mental health and social development in childhood and adolescence.

As in the mental health through sport conceptual model for adults described by Eather et al. (2023), the pathways linking sports participation to mental health outcomes are categorized into physical activity mechanisms and social mechanisms, each encompassing multiple mediating processes. These pathways are presented separately for conceptual clarity, but are expected to overlap and, in many cases, to be mutually reinforcing. For instance, fitness- and skill-related gains may enhance self-esteem and perceived competence, which in turn support peer relationships and a sense of belonging, while positive social experiences may promote motivation and persistence, increasing exposure to the physical benefits of sport. The present review also highlights developmental considerations that shape these pathways in young people, including distinct psychological mediators and potential differences in how sports influence well-being at various stages of development.

Physical activity pathway

The physical activity pathway aligns with established frameworks linking movement behaviors to psychological health. Lubans and colleagues proposed that physical activity improves mental health via neurobiological, psychosocial, and behavioral mechanisms [220]. Neurobiological mechanisms include changes in brain structure and function, stress regulation, and neuroplasticity, which contribute to emotional resilience and cognitive functioning. Psychosocial mechanisms involve changes in self-perception, self-efficacy, and self-esteem, while behavioral mechanisms refer to lifestyle adaptations, such as improved sleep and reduced sedentary behavior.

Consistent with this framework, the present review provides strong support for psychosocial mechanisms, particularly self-perception-related mediators (summarized in Table 3). We found consistent evidence that self-esteem [43], physical self-concept [81, 82], and body satisfaction [52] mediated the relationship between sports participation and improved psychological well-being. Cross-sectional studies show that young people who participate in sport report more favorable self-perceptions—such as higher sport-specific self-concept, physical self-esteem, and body satisfaction—which are associated with greater global self-esteem and fewer depressive symptoms [81, 82, 84, 131]. Longitudinal analyses extend this pattern by demonstrating that improvements in these self-perceptions predict subsequent gains in global self-esteem and reductions in depressive symptoms over time [108, 110]. Similarly, findings by White‐Gosselin, Poulin, and Denault [209] reinforce the centrality of psychosocial mediators, identifying self-efficacy, body image satisfaction, and global self-esteem as strong mediators of the relationship between physical activity and mental health.

Although less extensively studied than psychosocial mechanisms, behavioral regulation also appears to play a role in the mental health benefits of sport participation. In Lubans’ framework, behavioral pathways include adaptations such as improved sleep, reduced sedentary behavior, and healthier daily routines, which can support emotional and cognitive functioning [220]. In the present review, an experimental study by Shachar et al. [200] demonstrated that improvements in self-control through sport participation led to reductions in physical aggression, suggesting that sports may foster behavioral regulation. However, these findings are relatively sparse, and further research is needed to explore how behavioral adaptations contribute to psychological outcomes in young people.

Social pathway

The social pathway describes how sports participation influences mental health through social relationships, peer dynamics, and structured interpersonal interactions. According to Thoits (2011) [221], key social mechanisms include belonging and companionship, social support, social influence and comparison, role-based purpose and meaning, and social control.

The current review provides evidence for belonging and companionship, social support, and social influence/comparison as mediators linking sports participation to mental health outcomes (summarized in Table 3). This review found the strongest evidence for belonging and companionship as mediators of the benefits of sport, particularly team sports [54, 133, 152, 209, 212].These findings align with broader evidence emphasizing the importance of peer integration in fostering psychological resilience [222, 223].

Social support, including both peer and adult support, was found to increase self-esteem [98, 100, 104] and reduce depressive symptoms [131], highlighting the role of positive social interactions in youth sports settings. Findings from White‐Gosselin, Poulin, and Denault [130] further reinforce the importance of social support, showing strong evidence that social connection and belonging mediate the relationship between sport participation and mental health over time.

Social influence and comparison had both positive and negative effects, depending on the behaviors and characteristics of peers. Research by O’Donnell and Barber [161] indicated that affiliation with prosocial peers buffered against externalizing behaviors, whereas association with risk-taking peers predicted increased externalizing behaviors, underscoring the dual influence of peer dynamics in sports environments.

Other mechanisms proposed by Thoits (2011) [221], such as social control and role-based purpose and meaning, were not directly tested as mediators in the included studies but remain plausible pathways based on theoretical frameworks. Future research should examine whether structured expectations, responsibilities, and leadership roles within sport contribute to self-regulation and long-term mental health benefits.

Evidence for the benefits of sports over physical activity

A key contribution of this review was the inclusion of studies that explicitly distinguished between sport participation and general physical activity when examining mental health and social outcomes. Multiple studies demonstrated that the psychological benefits of sports participation extend beyond those attributable to physical activity alone, reinforcing the notion that sport-specific components—such as structured social interactions, goal setting, and skill mastery—may offer unique advantages. For example, one study found that physical activity accounted for less than a quarter of the mental health benefits associated with sport participation [72], highlighting the substantial contribution of non-physical elements of sport.

Similarly, Dishman and Hales [82] and McMahon and Corcoran [57] reported that while general physical activity was not consistently linked to reduced depressive symptoms, organized sport participation was supporting the view that sport represents a distinct and effective context for mental health promotion. Consistent with this, many studies in this review found stronger psychological benefits in team sports compared to individual sports, suggesting that the inherently social nature of team play may be particularly important for fostering positive mental health outcomes. Taken together, these findings indicate that sport offers unique mental health and social benefits beyond those conferred by physical activity alone, arising from its structured, socially embedded, and goal-directed nature. While physical activity remains fundamental for health, the evidence reviewed here suggests that the social and relational context inherent in sport may provide additional pathways to psychological benefit, consistent with the physical activity and social mechanisms outlined in the conceptual model.

Future directions and refinements to the model

This review supports an updated mental health through sport conceptual model for children and adolescents that integrates developmental considerations unique to children and adolescents. The model highlights the dual pathways of physical and social mechanisms, with self-perceptions, belonging, social support, and social influence emerging as key mediators of mental health benefits in youth. Importantly, the evidence suggests that sport—particularly team-based activities—offers psychological and social advantages beyond those of individual sports and general physical activity, likely due to its structured, goal-oriented, and socially interactive nature.

Despite the overall strength of the evidence, several important gaps and considerations specific to this population warrant further investigation. For children especially, developing motor competence may play a critical role in sustaining sport participation and fostering well-being, both as a prerequisite for continued engagement and as a source of direct psychological benefits [74, 224]. Similarly, the enjoyment of sport appears central to motivating participation in young people, who are more likely to engage for the immediate joy, challenge, and social interaction it provides rather than for long-term health outcomes [225–227].

Additional considerations include level of competition and gender differences, which may influence the mental health benefits of sport. While elite-level sport can build confidence and skills, it may also introduce unique stressors such as performance-related anxiety and burnout [228], particularly in children who specialize early [229]. Males and females may also differ in their motivations for participation, perceived benefits, and retention patterns, underscoring the need to design inclusive programs that accommodate these differences.

Future research should aim to rigorously test the mechanisms identified in this review, such as self-perceptions, belonging, social support, and social influence, using longitudinal and experimental designs that clarify their causal roles and potential dose–response relationships. At the same time, hypothesized but less-explored mechanisms, including motor competence, enjoyment, and the influence of coach and parent support, warrant investigation to determine their contributions to mental health outcomes. Clarifying how these mechanisms operate across developmental stages, sport types, and levels of competition will strengthen and refine the model. Such research will not only advance theoretical understanding but also inform the design of youth sport programs that maximize psychological and social benefits while minimizing potential risks.

Strengths and limitations

This systematic review offers several key strengths. First, it synthesizes a large and diverse body of research, incorporating 189 studies spanning cross-sectional, longitudinal, and experimental designs. Unlike previous reviews, which have relied primarily on cross-sectional evidence, this review includes a substantial number of longitudinal (50 studies) and experimental (18 studies) investigations, strengthening the ability to infer temporal and, in some cases, causal relationships between sports participation and mental health outcomes. Second, this review provides an updated and developmentally specific conceptual model of sports participation and mental health in children and adolescents. By building upon previous theoretical frameworks, the model integrates empirical findings that distinguish the pathways through which sport influences youth mental health, offering a structured foundation for future research and intervention design. Third, this review distinguishes between psychological well-being, psychological ill-being, prosocial behaviors, and antisocial behaviors, allowing for a more nuanced synthesis of outcomes than approaches that treat mental health as a unitary construct. Finally, this review highlights key mechanisms of action, highlighting mediators such as self-perceptions, social support, and peer belonging that help explain how sport participation is linked to mental health and social outcomes in young people.

Several limitations must also be acknowledged. First, although this review includes a substantial number of longitudinal and experimental studies, cross-sectional studies still made up a large proportion of the included research. While cross-sectional evidence consistently supports the positive association between sports participation and mental health, causality cannot be inferred from these designs alone. Further investigations, particularly experimental studies of organized sport programs across different sport types and levels of engagement, are needed to strengthen causal conclusions.

Second, heterogeneity in study design, sample characteristics, and measurement tools posed challenges for synthesis. Differences in how sports participation and mental health outcomes were operationalized may have contributed to inconsistencies across studies. Greater standardization in measurement and reporting would enhance comparability and strengthen future evidence syntheses.

Third, while this review examined multiple mental health and social outcomes, potential moderating factors—such as sport type, competition level, and intensity of participation—were inconsistently reported. This makes it difficult to determine the optimal conditions for maximizing mental health benefits. Additionally, although some studies considered the influence of sex, age, and socioeconomic status, further research is needed to clarify how these factors shape the psychological effects of sports participation.

Finally, research on the potential mental health and social harms of sport—such as excessive pressure from coaches, organizations, and parents; exclusion within teams; bullying; abuse; or restricted participation due to injury—remains understudied and underreported, particularly in quantitative and controlled study designs. These experiences, though not well captured in the existing evidence, can have serious, wide-ranging, and long-lasting consequences for young people’s mental and physical health. While the overall evidence reviewed here indicate that sport participation is generally beneficial, greater recognition of these risks is needed, along with proactive strategies to prevent harm and ensure that sport environments are violence-free, inclusive, and supportive so that the potential mental health benefits of sport can be realized for all participants.

Conclusion

This review provides substantial evidence that sports participation plays an important role in shaping the mental health and social outcomes of children and adolescents. The findings consistently highlight the benefits of sports participation for psychological well-being, with robust evidence supporting positive effects on self-esteem, life satisfaction, and quality of life. Likewise, there is compelling evidence that sports participation is associated with reduced psychological ill-being, particularly for depressive and anxiety symptoms. Beyond individual psychological benefits, sports participation fosters prosocial behaviors and enhances social support, belonging, and interpersonal relationships. Team sports, in particular, tended to show stronger associations with positive outcomes than individual sports, likely due to their structured and socially embedded nature.

This review also presents a refined “Mental Health through Sport” conceptual model for children and adolescents that synthesizes these findings and identifies key mechanisms—such as self-perceptions, social support, and peer belonging—through which sport influences psychological and social outcomes. The model highlights both physical activity and social pathways, with self-perceptions, social support, and peer belonging emerging as central mediators. Importantly, studies that directly compared sport participation with general physical activity indicated that sport can confer additional psychological benefits beyond those associated with physical activity alone, emphasizing the value of sport-specific social and psychological processes, particularly in team-based settings. Our model complements and extends prior work on physical activity and mental health mediators [230], and the work of Eather et al. (2024) on adult sport participation, by providing a developmental specific framework for youth in sport.

Future research should build on this foundation by employing more rigorous study designs, standardizing measurement approaches, and exploring key moderating factors such as sport type, competition level, and intensity of participation. Greater attention to potential harms, as well as to mechanisms such as motor competence, enjoyment, and coach and parent support, will further refine understanding of when and for whom sport is most beneficial. By clarifying how and why sports participation influences mental health and social outcomes in young people, this review provides a foundation for educators, policymakers, and practitioners to design and implement youth sport programs that maximize psychological and social benefits while minimizing risk.
